```latex
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 5 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A 2D plot showing the congruence level profile against height $t$. A horizontal axis labeled $t$ with an arrow to the right contains marked tick values $-3j$, $-j$, $0$, $j$, and $3j$. Labels $B_j$ appear above the axis at $-3j$ and $3j$. The curve forms a symmetric trapezoid: a straight line segment rises from $(-3j, 0)$ to $(-j, 2j)$ labeled $Q_{k_j(t)}$ along the incline; a horizontal segment runs at height $2j$ between $t = -j$ and $t = j$ labeled above by $Y_j \text{ (fiber } Q_{2j}\text{)}$; and a straight line segment descends from $(j, 2j)$ to $(3j, 0)$ labeled $Q_{k_j(t)}$ along the decline. On the left side, the height of the flat top is labeled $k_j(t) = 2j$. Two vertical dashed lines connect $t = -j$ to $(-j, 2j)$ and $t = j$ to $(j, 2j)$, with the enclosed region labeled "central collar".
```


The gradual reductions in Figure <a href="#fig:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:profile">1</a> have bounded fibers by Lemma <a href="#geom:quotients" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:quotients">2</a>. They therefore preserve a uniform degree bound even though the middle quotient grows rapidly with $j$.

<a id="stmt-geom-basic"></a>
<div id="geom:basic" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *The graphs $B_j,Y_j,X_j$ are finite and connected, with vertex degrees bounded independently of $j$. Their projections to $B_j$ are $1$-Lipschitz. Each end copy of $B_j$ is isometrically embedded in $X_j$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-basic"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-basic</summary>

*Proof.* Only the claims about $X_j$ remain to be checked. Consecutive slices are connected by surjective reduction maps, so $X_j$ is connected. A vertex has at most $|S|$ fiber neighbors, six base neighbors, and at most $2^9$ vertical neighbors on either side. Thus $|S|+6+2^{10}$ is a common bound. Projection sends an edge to a base edge or a point, proving the Lipschitz assertion. Within an end, every base path is available. Projection gives the opposite distance inequality, so the end metric is exactly the metric of $B_j$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Coarse disjoint unions

We now fix metrics on $$B=\bigsqcup_{j\geq1}B_j,\qquad
 Y=\bigsqcup_{j\geq1}Y_j,\qquad
 X=\bigsqcup_{j\geq1}X_j.$$ In $B_j$ choose the zero vertex, in $Y_j$ the vertex $(0,1)$, and in $X_j$ the vertex $(0,1)$ at height zero. In each union, join the chosen vertices of components $j$ and $j+1$ by an auxiliary edge of length $100+j^2$, and use the resulting path distances on the original vertex set. These edges define the metric; they add no vertices to the space.

A path which leaves and returns to the same component must pass through its chosen vertex both times. Consequently its use of the auxiliary edges cannot shorten an intrinsic component distance. At each fixed distance bound, cross-component pairs occur in only finitely many finite components. The resulting metrics are uniformly discrete and proper. They have bounded geometry because the weighted graphs have uniformly bounded degrees and all edge lengths are at least one: a ball of radius $R$ contains no more vertices than a ball of graph radius $\lfloor R\rfloor$ in a graph of the same degree bound.

Projection and end inclusion define maps <a id="geom:coarse-maps"></a>$$\label{geom:coarse-maps}
 f:Y\longrightarrow B,\qquad i_\pm:B\longrightarrow X.$$ On each component they are $1$-Lipschitz. For a fixed input distance bound, the remaining pairs lie in a finite set, so their image distances also have a finite bound. Thus the maps are bornologous. Preimages of bounded sets are finite, so the maps are proper. At scale $3$ none of the Rips complexes has a cross-component simplex. In particular, the end maps induce proper simplicial inclusions $$i_{\pm,3}:P_3(B)\longrightarrow P_3(X).$$ These are the maps used to form the K-homology class. The next section shows why its two endpoint contributions remain distinct at every fixed scale.

<a id="sec:persistence"></a>
### An integral class that persists at every scale

We construct a class $\beta\in\mathop{\mathrm{KX}}_1(B)$ whose images at the two ends of $X$ have an infinite-order difference. The obstruction to identifying these images will be integral: at any fixed Rips scale, an appropriate $K$-cohomology class becomes divisible by larger and larger powers of two over the middle graphs $Y_j$.

<a id="top:pairing-naturality"></a>
All $K$-groups in this section are complex and integral. For a finite complex $M$, we write $K^*(M)$ for topological $K$-theory and $K_*(M)=\mathop{\mathrm{KK}}_*(C(M),\mathbb C)$ for analytic $K$-homology. The pairing $$\langle\ ,\ \rangle:K^1(M)\times K_1(M)\longrightarrow\mathbb Z$$ is the Kasparov index pairing. In particular, for a continuous map $h:M\to N$ between finite complexes, $$\langle\eta,h_*\xi\rangle=\langle h^*\eta,\xi\rangle.
 \label{top:pairing-naturality}$$ We use the usual Mayer–Vietoris sequence for a finite complex covered by two subcomplexes; see <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[HigsonRoe00](#ref-HigsonRoe00)] for these $K$-homology constructions.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The classes on the two ends

Let $$T_j=(\mathbb R/l_j\mathbb Z)^3.$$ Give $T_j$ its unit cubical subdivision and triangulate the cubes periodically. The vertices are $B_j$, and the vertices of every cube have graph distance at most $3$. The resulting triangulated torus is therefore a subcomplex of $P_3(B_j)$; write $\kappa_j:T_j\hookrightarrow P_3(B_j)$ for its inclusion.

<a id="top:tau"></a>
Let $u_1,u_2,u_3\in H^1(T_j;\mathbb Z)$ be the positively oriented coordinate classes. Choose a line bundle $\mathcal L_j$ pulled back from the first two circles with $$c_1(\mathcal L_j)=u_1\smile u_2,$$ and let $e_3\in K^1(T_j)$ be the pullback of the positive generator of $K^1(S^1)$ under projection to the third circle. Define <a id="top:theta"></a>$$\theta_j=([\mathcal L_j]-1)e_3\in K^1(T_j).
 \label{top:theta}$$ This class is primitive. Indeed, the exterior-algebra description of $K^*((S^1)^3)$ identifies $[\mathcal L_j]-1$ with a generator of the reduced $K^0$ of the first two circles, and $\theta_j$ with the product of the three circle generators. Here the first assertion also follows from the first Chern class, which identifies $\widetilde K^0((S^1)^2)$ with $H^2((S^1)^2;\mathbb Z)$. The Künneth and universal coefficient theorems therefore give a class $$\tau_j\in K_1(T_j),\qquad
 \langle\theta_j,\tau_j\rangle=1.
 \label{top:tau}$$ More explicitly, $K^*(T_j)$ is free abelian, so the universal coefficient theorem identifies $K_1(T_j)$ with $\operatorname{Hom}(K^1(T_j),\mathbb Z)$, and a primitive element admits an integer-valued functional taking value $1$ on it; see <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Blackadar98](#ref-Blackadar98), Theorems 23.1.1 and 23.1.3].

<a id="top:kk-product"></a>
<a id="top:alpha"></a>
The component separation gives $P_3(B)=\bigsqcup_jP_3(B_j)$. Countable additivity in the first variable of $\mathop{\mathrm{KK}}$ gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{KK}}_1(C_0(P_3(B)),\mathbb C)
 \cong\prod_{j\geq1}K_1(P_3(B_j)).
 \label{top:kk-product}$$ This is a product, so the entire sequence $((\kappa_j)_*\tau_j)_j$ determines a class $\beta_3$ in the group on the left; see <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Blackadar98](#ref-Blackadar98), Theorem 19.7.1]. Write $\beta$ for its image in $\mathop{\mathrm{KX}}_1(B)$ and set $$\alpha_3=(i_{+,3})_*\beta_3-(i_{-,3})_*\beta_3,
 \qquad
 \alpha=[\alpha_3]\in\mathop{\mathrm{KX}}_1(X).
 \label{top:alpha}$$ Thus $\alpha=(i_+)_*\beta-(i_-)_*\beta$. We will prove that every nonzero integer multiple of $\alpha_3$ remains nonzero at every later Rips scale.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Retractions to the tori

Fix an integer $r\geq3$. From now on, thresholds on $j$ may depend on $r$, but not on a simplex or a vertex of the corresponding component.

<a id="stmt-top-interpolation"></a>
<div id="top:interpolation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *If $l_j>4r$, there is a continuous map $$p_{B,j}:P_r(B_j)\longrightarrow T_j$$ whose restriction to $T_j\subseteq P_3(B_j)\subseteq P_r(B_j)$ is the identity. The graph projections define continuous maps $$p_{Y,j}:P_r(Y_j)\longrightarrow T_j,
 \qquad
 p_{X,j}:P_r(X_j)\longrightarrow T_j$$ by composition with $p_{B,j}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-top-interpolation"><summary>Proof of stmt-top-interpolation</summary>

*Proof.* For vertices $u,v$ of a Rips simplex, each coordinate difference has a unique representative $\delta_i(u,v)\in\mathbb Z$ with $|\delta_i(u,v)|\leq r$. On a triangle $(u,v,w)$, the integer $$\delta_i(u,v)+\delta_i(v,w)-\delta_i(u,w)$$ is a multiple of $l_j$ and has absolute value at most $3r<l_j$; it therefore vanishes. Consequently the vertices of each simplex admit lifts to $\mathbb Z^3$ realizing all these differences. The lifts are unique up to a common element of $l_j\mathbb Z^3$. Affine interpolation, followed by reduction modulo $l_j\mathbb Z^3$, gives a map on the simplex independent of that choice. The maps agree on faces and define $p_{B,j}$.

On each simplex of the triangulated torus, this interpolation is its original parametrization, so the restriction is the identity. The projections $Y_j\to B_j$ and $X_j\to B_j$ are $1$-Lipschitz by Lemma <a href="#geom:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:basic">3</a>; hence they induce simplicial maps of the corresponding Rips complexes. ◻

</details>

The maps in Lemma <a href="#top:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="top:interpolation">4</a> allow us to compare the same torus class at the ends and in the middle. At either end its pairing with $\tau_j$ is $1$. In the middle, a congruence calculation forces divisibility.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The congruence calculation

<a id="stmt-top-chern-divisibility"></a>
<div id="top:chern-divisibility" class="lemma">

<a id="top:chern-divisibility-eq"></a>
<a id="top:line-root"></a>
**Lemma 5**. *For every fixed integer $r\geq3$ and all sufficiently large $j$, $$p_{Y,j}^*(u_1\smile u_2)
 \in 2^j H^2(P_r(Y_j);\mathbb Z).
 \label{top:chern-divisibility-eq}$$ Equivalently, there is a line bundle $\mathcal L'_{j,r}$ on $P_r(Y_j)$ such that $$p_{Y,j}^*\mathcal L_j
 \cong(\mathcal L'_{j,r})^{\otimes 2^j}.
 \label{top:line-root}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-top-chern-divisibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-top-chern-divisibility</summary>

*Proof.* Take $j$ large enough that $l_j>4r$ and reduction $\Gamma\to Q_{2j}$ is injective on the word ball of radius $2r$; the latter condition is available from Lemma <a href="#geom:quotients" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:quotients">2</a>. In this proof write $m=2^j$, suppress $j$ in $U_j,V_j$, and abbreviate $g_{j,k}$ to $g_k$.

We first describe integral cocycles representing $p_{Y,j}^*u_1,p_{Y,j}^*u_2$. For an oriented Rips edge $(x,y)$, let $v_x,v_y\in B_j$ be its projected endpoints and choose their coordinate representatives in $\{0,\ldots,l_j-1\}$. Define its first two seam numbers $a_{xy},b_{xy}\in\mathbb Z$ by $$\delta_1(v_x,v_y)=(v_y)_1-(v_x)_1+l_j a_{xy},
 \qquad
 \delta_2(v_x,v_y)=(v_y)_2-(v_x)_2+l_j b_{xy}.$$ The triangle identities in the proof of Lemma <a href="#top:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="top:interpolation">4</a> show that $a,b$ are cocycles. The seam numbers record the winding of the interpolated coordinate maps, so $[a]=p_{Y,j}^*u_1$ and $[b]=p_{Y,j}^*u_2$.

To prove divisibility by $m$, it suffices to show that $a\smile b$ becomes a coboundary modulo $m$. We will construct a $\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z$-valued $1$-cochain $c$ with $\delta c=-a\smile b$ by comparing edge transports after lifting from $Q_{2j}$ to $Q_{3j}$.

<a id="top:edge-transport"></a>
Order the vertices of $P_r(Y_j)$. For every edge $x<y$, choose a graph path from $x$ to $y$ of length at most $r$, and write $x=(v_x,q_x)$ and $y=(v_y,q_y)$. The left Cayley steps along this path give a word $w_{xy}\in\Gamma$ of length at most $r$. Left multiplications commute with all horizontal right multiplications. Moreover, $U,V$ commute modulo $m^2$, and the total seam numbers of the projected path are $a_{xy},b_{xy}$: its coordinate displacement is bounded by $r$ and therefore is the unique small lift used above. It follows that $$q_y=\overline w_{xy}\,q_x\,g_{2j}(a_{xy},b_{xy})
 \quad\text{in }Q_{2j}.
 \label{top:edge-transport}$$ Here a bar denotes reduction modulo $m^2$.

On an ordered triangle with vertices $x_0<x_1<x_2$, cocycle additivity and commutation modulo $m^2$ give $$g_{2j}(a_{01},b_{01})g_{2j}(a_{12},b_{12})
 =g_{2j}(a_{02},b_{02}).$$ Substitution in <a href="#top:edge-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:edge-transport">[top:edge-transport]</a> and cancellation give $\overline w_{02}=\overline w_{12}\overline w_{01}$. Both $w_{02}$ and $w_{12}w_{01}$ lie in the word ball of radius $2r$, so our injectivity assumption yields the equality in $\Gamma$: <a id="top:word-triangle"></a>$$w_{02}=w_{12}w_{01}.
 \label{top:word-triangle}$$

We now lift this calculation from modulus $m^2$ to modulus $m^3$. Every element of $$N=\ker(Q_{3j}\longrightarrow Q_{2j})$$ has the form $1+m^2D$ modulo $m^3$, with $D$ determined modulo $m$. Multiplication adds the matrices $D$, since $m^4$ is divisible by $m^3$. Thus $N$ is abelian and $$d:N\longrightarrow\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z,
 \qquad d(1+m^2D)=D_{13}\pmod m$$ is a homomorphism. Every element of $N$ commutes with every matrix that is $1$ modulo $m$, in particular with $$\widetilde g(a,b)=V^bU^a\pmod{m^3}.$$ Direct multiplication of the elementary matrices gives <a id="top:central-extension"></a>$$\begin{split}
 C&=UVU^{-1}V^{-1}=1+m^2E_{13}\pmod{m^3},
       \qquad d(C)=1,\\
 \widetilde g(a,b)\widetilde g(a',b')
   &=\widetilde g(a+a',b+b')C^{ab'}.
 \end{split}
 \label{top:central-extension}$$

<a id="top:lifted-edge"></a>
Choose a lift $\widetilde q_x\in Q_{3j}$ of each $q_x$, and denote the reduction of $w\in\Gamma$ to $Q_{3j}$ by $\widetilde w$. Equation <a href="#top:edge-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:edge-transport">[top:edge-transport]</a> defines $n_{xy}\in N$ by $$\widetilde q_y=
 \widetilde w_{xy}\widetilde q_x
 \widetilde g(a_{xy},b_{xy})n_{xy}.
 \label{top:lifted-edge}$$ For an ordered triangle, abbreviate $\widetilde g(a_{ab},b_{ab})$ to $\widetilde g_{ab}$. Substituting twice in <a href="#top:lifted-edge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:lifted-edge">[top:lifted-edge]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \widetilde q_2
 &=\widetilde w_{12}\widetilde w_{01}\widetilde q_0
       \widetilde g_{01}n_{01}\widetilde g_{12}n_{12}\\
 &=\widetilde w_{02}\widetilde q_0
       \widetilde g_{01}\widetilde g_{12}n_{01}n_{12}\\
 &=\widetilde w_{02}\widetilde q_0\widetilde g_{02}
       C^{a_{01}b_{12}}n_{01}n_{12}.
\end{aligned}$$ The second equality moves $n_{01}$ only past $\widetilde g_{12}$, which it commutes with; it uses no commutation with $\widetilde q_0$ or with a Cayley word. Comparing with the direct edge $02$ therefore gives $$n_{02}=C^{a_{01}b_{12}}n_{01}n_{12}.$$ Apply $d$ and put $c_{xy}=d(n_{xy})$. With the usual simplicial coboundary convention, this identity becomes $$(a\smile b)_{012}=a_{01}b_{12}
   =c_{02}-c_{01}-c_{12}=-(\delta c)_{012}
   \quad\text{in }\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z.$$ Hence the reduction of $[a\smile b]$ modulo $m$ is zero. The cohomology sequence of $$0\longrightarrow\mathbb Z\xrightarrow{\,m\,}\mathbb Z
   \longrightarrow\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z\longrightarrow0$$ identifies the kernel of reduction on $H^2$ with the image of multiplication by $m$. This proves <a href="#top:chern-divisibility-eq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:chern-divisibility-eq">[top:chern-divisibility-eq]</a>, without a torsion-freeness assumption on $H^2(P_r(Y_j);\mathbb Z)$. Finally, line bundles on a finite complex are classified by their integral first Chern class, and the first Chern class of a tensor power is the corresponding integer multiple. This proves <a href="#top:line-root" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:line-root">[top:line-root]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### From line-bundle roots to divisibility in $K^1$

For fixed $r$, bounded geometry supplies an integer $D_r\geq1$ such that $$\dim P_r(Y_j)\leq D_r\qquad(j\geq1).$$ Indeed, the vertices of a Rips simplex all belong to the radius-$r$ ball about any one of them, and these balls have uniformly bounded cardinality. The dimension bound converts the line-bundle roots into integral divisibility in $K$-theory.

<a id="stmt-top-k-divisibility"></a>
<div id="top:k-divisibility" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *For every fixed integer $r\geq3$ and all sufficiently large $j$, <a id="top:k-divisibility-eq"></a>$$p_{Y,j}^*\theta_j\in
 2^{\,j-\lfloor\log_2D_r\rfloor}K^1(P_r(Y_j)).
 \label{top:k-divisibility-eq}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-top-k-divisibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-top-k-divisibility</summary>

<a id="top:binomial"></a>
*Proof.* Take $j$ satisfying Lemma <a href="#top:chern-divisibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="top:chern-divisibility">5</a> and $2^j>D_r$, and put $z=[\mathcal L'_{j,r}]-1\in K^0(P_r(Y_j))$. The classifying map of this line bundle can be taken to land in $\mathbb{CP}^{D_r}$ by cellular approximation. The relation $([\mathcal H]-1)^{D_r+1}=0$ for the tautological line bundle $\mathcal H$ on $\mathbb{CP}^{D_r}$ therefore implies $z^{D_r+1}=0$; see <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Atiyah67](#ref-Atiyah67), Corollary 2.7.9]. Consequently <a href="#top:line-root" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:line-root">[top:line-root]</a> gives $$-1
 =(1+z)^{2^j}-1
 =\sum_{h=1}^{D_r}\binom{2^j}{h}z^h.
 \label{top:binomial}$$ For $1\leq h\leq D_r$, let $v_2(h)$ be the exponent of $2$ dividing $h$. The identity $$h\binom{2^j}{h}=2^j\binom{2^j-1}{h-1}$$ shows that $\binom{2^j}{h}$ is divisible by $2^{j-v_2(h)}$, hence by $2^{j-\lfloor\log_2D_r\rfloor}$. Every coefficient in <a href="#top:binomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:binomial">[top:binomial]</a> has this common divisor. Multiplying the resulting divisibility in $K^0$ by $p_{Y,j}^*e_3$ proves the claim. ◻

</details>

We have now obtained the growing divisor over the middle. It remains to show that an equality between the classes at the two ends would force their common pairing to have this divisor.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Mayer–Vietoris detection and the direct limit

For $r\geq3$, let $$t_{\pm,j,r}:T_j\longrightarrow P_r(X_j)$$ denote the inclusion of the triangulated torus at height $\pm3j$.

<a id="stmt-top-component-detection"></a>
<div id="top:component-detection" class="lemma">

<a id="top:component-nonzero"></a>
**Lemma 7**. *Fix an integer $r\geq3$ and a nonzero integer $n$. For all sufficiently large $j$, $$n(t_{+,j,r})_*\tau_j-n(t_{-,j,r})_*\tau_j
 \neq0\quad\text{in }K_1(P_r(X_j)).
 \label{top:component-nonzero}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-top-component-detection"><summary>Proof of stmt-top-component-detection</summary>

*Proof.* In addition to the hypotheses needed for Lemma <a href="#top:k-divisibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="top:k-divisibility">6</a>, take $j>2r$. Cover $P_r(X_j)$ by the induced subcomplexes $$M^+=P_r(X_j)|_{\{t\geq-r\}},\qquad
 M^-=P_r(X_j)|_{\{t\leq r\}},$$ and put $I=M^+\cap M^-$. These subcomplexes cover: a simplex not contained in either would contain vertices at heights below $-r$ and above $r$, contradicting its diameter bound because height is $1$-Lipschitz. Their intersection is induced on the vertices at heights in $[-r,r]$.

There is a simplicial map $$h:I\longrightarrow P_r(Y_j)$$ that forgets height. To check this, join any two vertices in a simplex of $I$ by a graph path of length at most $r$ in $X_j$. Every vertex of this path has height in $[-2r,2r]$, which lies in the product collar since $j>2r$. Forgetting height sends the path to a path in $Y_j$ of no greater length. Thus the images of the simplex vertices have diameter at most $r$. The interpolation maps satisfy <a id="top:collar-factorization"></a>$$p_{X,j}|_I=p_{Y,j}\circ h.
 \label{top:collar-factorization}$$

<a id="top:divisibility-contradiction"></a>
Let $a_\pm:I\hookrightarrow M^\pm$ and $b_\pm:M^\pm\hookrightarrow P_r(X_j)$ be the inclusions, and write $t^\pm:T_j\to M^\pm$ for the endpoint inclusions. The relevant part of the Mayer–Vietoris sequence is $$K_1(I)\xrightarrow{((a_+)_*,-(a_-)_*)}
 K_1(M^+)\oplus K_1(M^-)
 \xrightarrow{(b_+)_*+(b_-)_*}K_1(P_r(X_j)).$$ If the difference in <a href="#top:component-nonzero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:component-nonzero">[top:component-nonzero]</a> were zero, exactness would give $\eta\in K_1(I)$ with $$(a_+)_*\eta=n(t^+)_*\tau_j,
 \qquad (a_-)_*\eta=n(t^-)_*\tau_j.$$ Pair the first equality with $(p_{X,j}|_{M^+})^*\theta_j$. Since $p_{X,j}\circ t_{+,j,r}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_{T_j}$, naturality <a href="#top:pairing-naturality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:pairing-naturality">[top:pairing-naturality]</a> and <a href="#top:tau" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:tau">[top:tau]</a> give $$n
 =\langle(p_{X,j}|_I)^*\theta_j,\eta\rangle
 =\langle h^*p_{Y,j}^*\theta_j,\eta\rangle.
 \label{top:divisibility-contradiction}$$ By Lemma <a href="#top:k-divisibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="top:k-divisibility">6</a>, the last integer is divisible by $2^{j-\lfloor\log_2D_r\rfloor}$. Taking $j$ large enough that $$j-\lfloor\log_2D_r\rfloor>v_2(|n|)$$ contradicts <a href="#top:divisibility-contradiction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:divisibility-contradiction">[top:divisibility-contradiction]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-top-infinite-order"></a>
<div id="top:infinite-order" class="proposition">

**Proposition 8**. *The class $\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{KX}}_1(X)$ defined in <a href="#top:alpha" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:alpha">[top:alpha]</a> has infinite order. In particular, $\alpha\otimes1\neq0$ in $\mathop{\mathrm{KX}}_1(X)\otimes\mathbb Q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-top-infinite-order"><summary>Proof of stmt-top-infinite-order</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a nonzero integer $n$ and an integer $r\geq3$. Let $\alpha_r$ be the image of $\alpha_3$ under the map induced by $P_3(X)\hookrightarrow P_r(X)$. For all sufficiently large $j$, the component separation ensures that no simplex of $P_r(X)$ meets both $X_j$ and its complement. Thus $P_r(X_j)$ is a clopen subcomplex, and extension by zero on functions gives a $*$-homomorphism $$C(P_r(X_j))\longrightarrow C_0(P_r(X)).$$ Contravariance of $\mathop{\mathrm{KK}}$ in its first variable defines restriction $$\mathop{\mathrm{KK}}_1(C_0(P_r(X)),\mathbb C)\longrightarrow K_1(P_r(X_j)).$$ By <a href="#top:kk-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:kk-product">[top:kk-product]</a> and functoriality, the restriction of $n\alpha_r$ is exactly the difference in <a href="#top:component-nonzero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:component-nonzero">[top:component-nonzero]</a>. Choosing $j$ also large enough for Lemma <a href="#top:component-detection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="top:component-detection">7</a> proves $n\alpha_r\neq0$.

We have proved that for every nonzero $n$ and every integer $r\geq3$, the scale-$r$ image of $n\alpha_3$ is nonzero. If $n\alpha$ vanished in the filtered direct limit defining $\mathop{\mathrm{KX}}_1(X)$, it would vanish at some finite later scale; integer scales are cofinal, so this is impossible. Hence $\alpha$ has infinite order. The kernel of the map from any abelian group to its tensor product with $\mathbb Q$ is its torsion subgroup, which proves the final assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:maximal-index"></a>
### Maximal Roe algebras and the coarse index

The infinite-order class is now constructed. It remains to show that its two end contributions have the same maximal index. The subsequent averaging and sliding arguments will use operator formulas on the standard modules. We therefore establish the norm estimates that allow those formulas to be used in the maximal completion, and then verify the required naturality of the index. These are standard features of maximal Roe theory; see <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[OOY09](#ref-OOY09), Section 2] and <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[SS21](#ref-SS21), Section 2]. We give the details needed here because the distinction between operator norm and maximal norm is essential to the proof.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Norm control and multipliers

Let $Z$ be a uniformly discrete proper metric space of bounded geometry, and write $$H_0=\ell^2(\mathbb N),\qquad H_Z=\ell^2(Z)\otimes H_0,
 \qquad A_Z=C^*_{\max}(Z).$$ The algebra $\mathbb C[Z]$ consists of the bounded finite-propagation operators on $H_Z$ that are locally compact for the multiplication representation of $C_0(Z)$. We denote their concrete operator norm by $\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ and their maximal norm by $\|\cdot\|_{\max}$. Write $T_{xy}:H_0\to H_0$ for the matrix entries of an operator $T$.

For a bounded finite-propagation operator, local compactness is equivalent to compactness of every entry $T_{xy}$. In one direction, compress by the characteristic functions of single vertices. Conversely, every row and column of a finite-propagation matrix has only finitely many entries. Multiplication on either side by a finitely supported function therefore gives a finite matrix of compact operators. Approximation by finitely supported functions proves the assertion for every function in $C_0(Z)$. In particular, no uniform bound on the ranks, or on finite-rank approximations to the entries, is part of this definition.

<a id="stmt-ana-fixed-propagation"></a>
<div id="ana:fixed-propagation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Fixed-propagation norm control). *For every $R\geq 0$ there is a constant $c_Z(R)<\infty$ such that $$\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|T\|_{\max}
 \leq c_Z(R)\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \qquad(T\in\mathbb C[Z],\ \mathop{\mathrm{prop}}(T)\leq R).$$ Consequently, operator-norm convergence of algebraic Roe operators with one common propagation bound implies convergence in maximal norm.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-fixed-propagation"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-fixed-propagation</summary>

*Proof.* Put $N_R=\sup_{z\in Z}|B_Z(z,R)|$. The support of $T$, viewed as a bipartite graph with a row copy and a column copy of $Z$, has degree at most $N_R$. A greedy edge coloring partitions it into at most $2N_R-1$ matchings. Accordingly, $T=\sum_{a=1}^{2N_R-1}T^{(a)}$, where each $T^{(a)}$ has at most one nonzero entry in each row and column, and $\|T^{(a)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}$.

The diagonal algebra $$\prod_{z\in Z}\mathcal K(H_0)
 =\left\{\operatorname{diag}(k_z): k_z\in\mathcal K(H_0),\
                  \sup_z\|k_z\|<\infty\right\}$$ is a $C^*$-subalgebra of $\mathbb C[Z]$. Every $*$-representation of $\mathbb C[Z]$ restricts to a contractive representation of this algebra. Since $(T^{(a)})^*T^{(a)}$ is diagonal, every such representation $\pi$ satisfies $$\|\pi(T^{(a)})\|^2
 \leq\|(T^{(a)})^*T^{(a)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \leq\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2.$$ The upper bound follows with $c_Z(R)=2N_R-1$. The concrete representation on $H_Z$ gives the lower bound. Apply the estimate to differences to obtain the convergence assertion. ◻

</details>

This matching argument is the discrete-module form of the maximal-norm estimate in <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[OOY09](#ref-OOY09), Lemma 2.1]; compare <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[SW13](#ref-SW13), Lemma 1.10]. We shall also use the following stronger statement when the vertex support, rather than merely the propagation, is fixed.

<a id="stmt-ana-compacts"></a>
<div id="ana:compacts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (The compact ideal). *An operator in $\mathbb C[Z]$ supported on $F\times F$, for a finite set $F\subset Z$, has the same maximal and concrete norms. The closure in $A_Z$ of all such operators is an ideal $\mathcal K_Z$, canonically isomorphic to $\mathcal K(H_Z)$. In particular, $K_1(\mathcal K_Z)=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-compacts"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-compacts</summary>

*Proof.* The operators supported on $F\times F$ form the $C^*$-algebra $\mathcal K(\ell^2(F)\otimes H_0)$. Restriction of any representation is contractive, and the concrete representation gives the reverse inequality. These finite-vertex algebras have dense union in $\mathcal K(H_Z)$. Multiplying one of their elements on either side by a finite-propagation operator with compact entries gives another compact operator of finite vertex support: only a finite metric neighborhood of $F$ can occur. Density proves the ideal assertion. ◻

</details>

We will need finite-propagation operators whose entries are bounded but need not be compact, such as the unitaries implementing the group action. Here and below $M(A_Z)$ denotes the multiplier algebra of $A_Z$.

<a id="stmt-ana-multipliers"></a>
<div id="ana:multipliers" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Finite-propagation multipliers). *Every bounded finite-propagation operator $D$ on $H_Z$ acts by left and right multiplication on $\mathbb C[Z]$, and these actions extend to a multiplier of $A_Z$. This assignment is a faithful $*$-homomorphism and satisfies $$\|D\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq \|D\|_{M(A_Z)}
 \leq c_Z(R)\|D\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \qquad\text{if }\mathop{\mathrm{prop}}(D)\leq R.$$ For an operator supported on a single matching, the multiplier norm equals the concrete norm.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-multipliers"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-multipliers</summary>

*Proof.* Products of $D$ with elements of $\mathbb C[Z]$ have finite propagation and compact entries. Decompose $D$ into matchings as in Lemma <a href="#ana:fixed-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:fixed-propagation">9</a>. It suffices to show that a matching operator whose entries have norm at most one acts contractively. For such an operator, $D^*D$ is a diagonal positive contraction. The operator $W=(1-D^*D)^{1/2}$ is bounded and diagonal. For $T\in\mathbb C[Z]$, all terms in $$T^*T-(DT)^*(DT)=(WT)^*(WT)$$ belong to $\mathbb C[Z]$. Applying each $*$-representation gives $\|DT\|_{\max}\leq\|T\|_{\max}$. Applying this to $D^*$ and taking adjoints gives the corresponding right-multiplication bound. The algebraic compatibility identities between these left and right actions pass to the completion, so they define a multiplier. Summing the matching estimates gives the asserted upper bound.

The assignment preserves products and adjoints on the dense algebra $\mathbb C[Z]$. The concrete representation of $A_Z$ on $H_Z$ is nondegenerate, since it contains $\mathcal K(H_Z)$, and hence extends to the multiplier algebra. It sends the multiplier just constructed to the original operator $D$, proving the lower bound and faithfulness. ◻

</details>

In particular, an operator identity involving bounded finite-propagation operators remains an identity between their multipliers. This statement does not assert that the concrete representation of the entire maximal Roe algebra is faithful. The finite-propagation control in the preceding lemmas is what will justify the specific limits below.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Maps induced by coarse maps

A map $h:Z\to W$ between uniformly discrete proper bounded-geometry spaces is coarse if inverse images of bounded sets are bounded and, for every $R$, there is $S$ such that $d_Z(x,y)\leq R$ implies $d_W(h(x),h(y))\leq S$. Choose an isometry $V_h:H_Z\to H_W$ that sends the summand over $z$ into the summand over $h(z)$, using orthogonal ranges when several vertices have the same image. Such a choice is possible because the vertex fibers are finite and $H_0$ is infinite dimensional. We call $V_h$ an exact covering isometry for $h$.

<a id="stmt-ana-functoriality"></a>
<div id="ana:functoriality" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12** (Coarse functoriality). *Conjugation by an exact covering isometry defines a $*$-homomorphism $$h_A=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(V_h):A_Z\longrightarrow A_W.$$ Its induced map on $K$-theory is independent of the covering isometry. These induced maps respect composition, and an exact covering isometry over $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_Z$ induces the identity on $K_*(A_Z)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-functoriality"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-functoriality</summary>

*Proof.* For $T\in\mathbb C[Z]$, coarse control bounds the propagation of $V_hTV_h^*$. Each of its vertex entries is a finite sum of compact operators, because $h^{-1}(w)$ is finite for each $w$. Thus conjugation is a $*$-homomorphism $\mathbb C[Z]\to\mathbb C[W]$, and the universal property extends it contractively to the maximal completions.

For two choices $V_0,V_1$, put $$V_\lambda=
 \begin{pmatrix}\cos\lambda\,V_0\\ \sin\lambda\,V_1\end{pmatrix}
 :H_Z\longrightarrow H_W\oplus H_W,
 \qquad 0\leq\lambda\leq\pi/2.$$ Each $V_\lambda$ is an isometry. Conjugation defines a homomorphism to $M_2(A_W)$: its four matrix entries have compact vertex entries and one common propagation bound for every fixed $T\in\mathbb C[Z]$. Moreover, $$\|V_\lambda T V_\lambda^*-V_\nu T V_\nu^*\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \leq 2\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}|\lambda-\nu|.$$ Lemma <a href="#ana:fixed-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:fixed-propagation">9</a>, applied to the four entries, promotes this estimate to maximal-norm continuity on $\mathbb C[Z]$; contractivity and density give continuity on $A_Z$. The two endpoint homomorphisms are $\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(V_0)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(V_1)$ in the two standard matrix corners, which induce the same stabilized $K$-theory map. Composing covering isometries proves functoriality, and comparison with the identity isometry proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

This is the exact-covering version of <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[OOY09](#ref-OOY09), Lemma 2.15]. For the maps in our construction we henceforth choose such homomorphisms and write $$f_A:A_Y\longrightarrow A_B,\qquad
 i_{\pm,A}:A_B\longrightarrow A_X.$$ By Proposition <a href="#ana:functoriality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:functoriality">12</a>, all statements about their $K$-theory maps are independent of these choices.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The canonical odd index and naturality

The remaining index-theoretic input is compatibility with the two end inclusions.

<a id="stmt-ana-naturality"></a>
<div id="ana:naturality" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13** (Naturality for the end classes). *For the classes $\beta\in\mathop{\mathrm{KX}}_1(B)$ and $\alpha=(i_+)_*\beta-(i_-)_*\beta\in\mathop{\mathrm{KX}}_1(X)$ constructed above, <a id="eq:ana-naturality"></a>$$\label{eq:ana-naturality}
 \mu_X^{\max}(\alpha)
 =(i_{+,A})_*\mu_B^{\max}(\beta)
  -(i_{-,A})_*\mu_B^{\max}(\beta).$$*

</div>

To verify this identity in the maximal completion, we first identify the Roe algebra of a Rips complex with the vertex algebra and then recall the exponential formula for its canonical odd index.

For $r>0$, regard a point of $P_r(Z)$ as a finitely supported probability measure on $Z$ whose support has diameter at most $r$. Equip the complex with the $1$-Wasserstein metric induced by $d_Z$. Every point is within $r$ of every vertex of its support. Moreover, a bounded set in this metric uses only vertices from one bounded subset of $Z$: if $v$ is a support vertex of $p$, then $$d_Z(v,z_0)\leq r+\int_Z d_Z(z,z_0)\,dp(z)
             =r+d_{W_1}(p,\delta_{z_0}).$$ That subset is finite. Consequently this metric is proper and induces the usual topology on the locally finite complex. The vertex inclusion and any selection of a support vertex are coarse inverses.

Choose a countable dense subset $E\subset P_r(Z)$ containing the vertices, and use the ample module $\ell^2(E)\otimes H_0$ with pointwise multiplication by $C_0(P_r(Z))$. Assign each point of $E$ to one vertex of its support, assigning a vertex to itself. This partitions the module into infinite-dimensional separable blocks $H_v$, one for each $v\in Z$; choose identifications $H_v\cong H_0$.

Under this regrouping, the algebraic Roe algebra of $P_r(Z)$ is exactly $\mathbb C[Z]$. Propagation bounds in the two descriptions differ by at most $2r$. For local compactness, the points in $H_v$ lie in the compact closed star of $v$, so a compactly supported continuous cutoff equal to one on that star shows that a locally compact operator has compact vertex-block entries. Conversely, a compact subset of $P_r(Z)$ meets only finitely many blocks. Compact vertex-block entries and finite propagation therefore give local compactness after multiplication by a compactly supported function, and then by any function in $C_0(P_r(Z))$. The algebraic identification extends to the maximal completions by universality. It is the coarse identification induced by the chosen support-vertex map. Pseudolocality below is always taken relative to $C_0(P_r(Z))$; the regrouping is used only for the Roe algebra and for Lemma <a href="#ana:multipliers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:multipliers">11</a>.

We recall the cycle description of the odd index. On an ample module for $P_r(Z)$, an odd $K$-homology class is represented, after stabilization and addition of degenerate cycles, by a bounded self-adjoint operator $F$ such that $$[F,a]\text{ is compact},\qquad a(F^2-1)\text{ is compact}
 \quad(a\in C_0(P_r(Z))).$$ The first condition is pseudolocality. The use of a fixed ample module is the usual Paschke realization of locally finite $K$-homology; see <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[HigsonRoe00](#ref-HigsonRoe00)].

Such a representative may be taken to have finite propagation. Indeed, let $\varphi_v$ be the square roots of the barycentric coordinate functions. Their supports are compact, have diameter at most $2r$, and form a locally finite family with $\sum_v\varphi_v^2=1$. Set $$F'=\sum_v\varphi_vF\varphi_v.$$ The sum converges strongly and has norm at most $\|F\|$: it is the compression of the direct sum of copies of $F$ by the isometry $\xi\mapsto(\varphi_v\xi)_v$. It has propagation at most $2r$. Furthermore, $$F'-F=\sum_v\varphi_v[F,\varphi_v]$$ is locally compact, since multiplication on the left by a compactly supported function leaves only finitely many compact summands; use self-adjointness for the right-hand assertion. Thus the straight-line path from $F$ to $F'$ consists of cycles and does not change the $K$-homology class. Replace $F$ by $F'$.

<a id="eq:ana-odd-index"></a>
After regrouping into vertex blocks, Lemma <a href="#ana:multipliers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:multipliers">11</a> makes $F$ a self-adjoint multiplier of $A_Z$, and finite propagation gives $F^2-1\in\mathbb C[Z]$. Hence $$p=\frac{1+F}{2}\in M(A_Z),\qquad
 p^2-p\in\mathbb C[Z],\qquad
 u_F=\exp(2\pi i p)\in 1+A_Z.$$ The odd maximal index is $$\label{eq:ana-odd-index}
 \operatorname{Ind}_{Z,\max}(F)=[u_F]\in K_1(A_Z).$$ To relate this formula to the canonical index, let $D^*_{\max}(P_r(Z))$ be the universal completion of the algebra of finite-propagation pseudolocal operators on the Rips module. It contains $A_Z$ as an ideal, and Paschke duality identifies $$\mathop{\mathrm{KK}}_1(C_0(P_r(Z)),\mathbb C)
 \cong K_0\bigl(D^*_{\max}(P_r(Z))/A_Z\bigr),
 \qquad [F]\longmapsto[p\bmod A_Z].$$ These assertions, including existence of the universal completion, are proved in <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[SS21](#ref-SS21), Proposition 2.22, Propositions 2.27–2.28, and Corollary 2.29]. The index is the boundary map of this extension. The multiplier action of Lemma <a href="#ana:multipliers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:multipliers">11</a> agrees with the inclusion on $A_Z$, so its exponential boundary formula is exactly <a href="#eq:ana-odd-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ana-odd-index">[eq:ana-odd-index]</a>. Thus this formula computes the canonical index, with its usual boundary-map sign convention. It agrees with the cycle construction in <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[OOY09](#ref-OOY09), Corollary 2.18]. Passing to the Rips direct limit gives $\mu_Z^{\max}$.

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-naturality"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-naturality</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#ana:naturality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:naturality">13</a>.* Both classes are represented at Rips scale $3$, where the end maps are proper continuous coarse simplicial inclusions $i_{\pm,3}:P_3(B)\to P_3(X)$. Choose the dense-set modules compatibly: the target dense set contains the images of the source dense set, and the support-vertex choices agree on these images. The resulting module isometry $V$ is an exact covering isometry for the end map after regrouping.

If $F$ represents a source odd class and has finite propagation, its push-forward is represented on the target module by $$\widetilde F=VFV^*-(1-VV^*).$$ The complement is a degenerate cycle. Properness ensures that pullbacks of $C_0$ functions lie in $C_0$, so $\widetilde F$ is pseudolocal and $\widetilde F^2-1$ is locally compact. Coarse control preserves finite propagation. Writing $p=(1+F)/2$, we have $\widetilde p=(1+\widetilde F)/2=VpV^*$.

For completeness, compatibility of the exponential with conjugation holds in the maximal completion itself. The function $$h(z)=\frac{\exp(2\pi i z)-1}{z^2-z}$$ extends to an entire function. Approximate $h$ uniformly by polynomials on disks containing the spectra of the two multipliers. Since $$\exp(2\pi i p)-1=(p^2-p)h(p),$$ and $p^2-p\in\mathbb C[B]$, all polynomial identities are identities on the algebraic Roe algebras, and their limits hold in maximal norm. They give $$\exp(2\pi i\widetilde p)-1
   =i_{\pm,A}\bigl(\exp(2\pi i p)-1\bigr).$$ Thus the two indices commute with the end inclusion. Apply this to $\beta_3$, subtract the two identities, and use the definition of the Rips direct-limit assembly map. Independence of the covering choices follows from Proposition <a href="#ana:functoriality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:functoriality">12</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:transfer"></a>
### A transfer in the maximal Roe algebra

The projection $f:Y\to B$ forgets a finite Cayley graph over each base vertex. We will lift operators on $B$ to operators on $Y$ by transporting their matrix entries between these fibers and then projecting onto the constant vectors. The resulting map supplies the analytic class that we will move from one end of $X$ to the other.

<a id="stmt-ana-transfer"></a>
<div id="ana:transfer" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14**. *For the projection $f:Y\to B$ there is a $*$-homomorphism $$\Phi:A_B\longrightarrow A_Y$$ such that $(f_A\Phi)_*=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$ on $K_*(A_B)$. In particular, $$(f_A)_*:K_1(A_Y)\longrightarrow K_1(A_B)$$ is surjective.*

</div>

Throughout this section, $u,v,w$ denote vertices of $B$. Write $F_u=f^{-1}(u)$ and let $$\xi_u=|F_u|^{-1/2}\sum_{y\in F_u}\delta_y
 \quad\text{in }\ell^2(F_u).$$ These vectors define an isometry <a id="eq:constant-isometry"></a>$$\label{eq:constant-isometry}
 V:H_B\longrightarrow H_Y,
 \qquad V(\delta_u\otimes h)=\xi_u\otimes h.$$ The diameters of the fibers are not uniformly bounded, so $V$ itself need not be a covering isometry for a coarse map. We will obtain an element of the maximal Roe algebra from this averaging operation by using property <span class="upright">(T)</span>.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The projection onto fiber constants

For $\gamma\in\Gamma$, let $U_\gamma$ act by left multiplication by $\gamma$ on every fiber, with the identity on $H_0$. This is a unitary operator of propagation at most the word length of $\gamma$. Lemma <a href="#ana:multipliers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:multipliers">11</a> therefore gives a unitary representation $$\Gamma\longrightarrow M(A_Y),\qquad\gamma\longmapsto U_\gamma,$$ and hence a unital $*$-homomorphism from $C^*_{\max}(\Gamma)$ into $M(A_Y)$.

<a id="eq:kazhdan-convergence"></a>
We recall explicitly the consequence of property <span class="upright">(T)</span> that will be used; see <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[BHV08](#ref-BHV08)]. In $C^*_{\max}(\Gamma)$ put $$\Delta=1-\frac1{|S|}\sum_{s\in S}s.$$ For every unitary representation $\pi$ and every vector $\eta$, $$\langle\pi(\Delta)\eta,\eta\rangle
   =\frac1{2|S|}\sum_{s\in S}
      \|\pi(s)\eta-\eta\|^2.$$ In particular, $0\le\Delta\le2$, and the kernel of $\pi(\Delta)$ is the invariant subspace. Property <span class="upright">(T)</span>, applied to the fixed finite generating set $S$, gives $\kappa>0$ such that every unit vector orthogonal to that subspace is moved by at least $\kappa$ by some $s\in S$. Consequently, with $\varepsilon=\kappa^2/(2|S|)$, $$\sigma_{C^*_{\max}(\Gamma)}(\Delta)
     \subseteq\{0\}\cup[\varepsilon,2].$$ The continuous functional calculus thus gives a projection onto the invariant vectors in every representation, and $$\left(1-\frac{\Delta}{2}\right)^n$$ converges to this projection in the maximal group norm. Denote its image in $M(A_Y)$ by $P$, and denote the images of the displayed approximants by $P_n$. We have $$\label{eq:kazhdan-convergence}
 \|P_n-P\|_{M(A_Y)}\longrightarrow0.$$ Each $P_n$ is a finite-propagation multiplier preserving every fiber.

In the concrete representation on $H_Y$, the invariant vectors in $\ell^2(F_u)$ are precisely the constant vectors: the map $\Gamma\to Q_{2j}$ is onto and its left action on $F_u$ is transitive. Thus the concrete image of $P$ is $VV^*$. The transfer itself will use $P$ in the maximal multiplier algebra $M(A_Y)$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Transporting matrix entries

<a id="eq:transport-constants"></a>
For each pair $u,v$ in the same component $B_j$, choose a shortest edge path from $v$ to $u$, choosing the reverse path for the reversed pair. Horizontal transport along this path is a permutation $$O_{uv}:\ell^2(F_v)\longrightarrow\ell^2(F_u).$$ Take $O_{uu}=1$. When $u,v$ lie in different components, instead put $$O_{uv}=|\xi_u\rangle\langle\xi_v|.$$ In both cases, $$\label{eq:transport-constants}
 O_{vu}=O_{uv}^*,\qquad
 O_{uv}\xi_v=\xi_u,\qquad O_{uv}^*\xi_u=\xi_v.$$ Moreover, $O_{uv}$ intertwines the left $\Gamma$-actions. For horizontal transport this follows because the matchings use right multiplication; for the rank-one operators it follows because both vectors are invariant.

<a id="eq:transport-lift"></a>
Given $T\in\mathbb C[B]$, use the decomposition $H_Y=\bigoplus_{u\in B}\ell^2(F_u)\otimes H_0$ to define $$\label{eq:transport-lift}
 L(T)_{uv}=O_{uv}\otimes T_{uv}.$$ This is a linear, adjoint-preserving lift, but it need not be multiplicative. The next lemma isolates both its control and the precise failure of multiplicativity.

<a id="stmt-ana-transport-defect"></a>
<div id="ana:transport-defect" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:transport-defect"></a>
**Lemma 15**. *For $T\in\mathbb C[B]$, the operator $L(T)$ belongs to $\mathbb C[Y]$ and commutes with $P$. For $T,T'\in\mathbb C[B]$, the defect $$\label{eq:transport-defect}
 D=L(T)L(T')-L(TT')$$ has finite vertex support and satisfies $PD=0$ in $A_Y$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-transport-defect"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-transport-defect</summary>

*Proof.* Let $R$ be a propagation bound for $T$. The matrix support of $T$ can be partitioned into finitely many matchings of the base vertices, with a number depending only on $R$ and the bounded geometry of $B$. On one such matching, the blocks in <a href="#eq:transport-lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-lift">[eq:transport-lift]</a> have disjoint domain and range blocks, and their norms are at most $\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}$. Summing over the matchings proves that $L(T)$ is bounded.

Within a component, any nonzero vertex entry of $O_{uv}$ joins vertices connected by the chosen horizontal path. Its length is $d_B(u,v)\le R$. Among different components, only finitely many vertex pairs $(u,v)$ can occur at this propagation bound; their fibers are finite. The remaining entries therefore also have a finite propagation bound. Each vertex entry of $L(T)$ is a scalar multiple of a compact operator $T_{uv}$. It follows that $L(T)\in\mathbb C[Y]$. The intertwining property of $O_{uv}$ shows that $L(T)$ commutes with every $U_\gamma$, hence with every $P_n$ and with $P$.

We next locate the defect. Let $R'$ be a propagation bound for $T'$. Outside finitely many components, all entries involved in <a href="#eq:transport-defect" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-defect">[eq:transport-defect]</a> stay in a single component $B_j$. For a summand with intermediate vertex $v$, the concatenated path from $w$ to $v$ to $u$ has length at most $R+R'$, as does the chosen path from $w$ to $u$. If $l_j>2(R+R')$, these two paths have the same total seam crossing numbers. Indeed, their lifted coordinate displacements differ by a multiple of $l_j$ and have difference of absolute value at most $2(R+R')$ in each coordinate. That multiple must be zero. Since $U_j$ and $V_j$ commute in $Q_{2j}$, horizontal transport depends only on these seam totals. It follows that $$O_{uv}O_{vw}=O_{uw}$$ for every summand under consideration. The defect therefore vanishes on all sufficiently large components. The omitted components and any cross-component entries lie in a finite union of finite graphs, so $D$ has finite vertex support.

Equations <a href="#eq:transport-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-constants">[eq:transport-constants]</a> give, on the concrete Hilbert spaces, $$L(T)V=VT,\qquad V^*L(T)=TV^*.$$ The concrete image of $PD$ is consequently zero. To obtain the equality in the maximal completion, choose a finite union $E$ of components of $Y$ containing the vertex support of $D$. Every $P_n$ preserves each fiber, so $P_nD$ has vertex support in the same finite set $E$. Lemma <a href="#ana:compacts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:compacts">10</a> identifies its maximal norm with its concrete operator norm. On $H_Y$, we have $P_nD\to0$ in operator norm; hence $P_nD\to0$ in maximal norm. On the other hand, <a href="#eq:kazhdan-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kazhdan-convergence">[eq:kazhdan-convergence]</a> gives $P_nD\to PD$ in $A_Y$. Thus $PD=0$ in $A_Y$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The splitting

We can now define the transfer and compute its composition with $f_A$. The first task used finite vertex support. The second will use a different control: averaging can increase propagation inside a fiber, but it does not increase propagation after the fiber is projected to its base vertex.

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-transport-defect-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-transport-defect</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#ana:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:transfer">14</a>.* For $T\in\mathbb C[B]$, put $$\Phi(T)=PL(T)\in A_Y.$$ Since $P$ is a projection commuting with the lifts, Lemma <a href="#ana:transport-defect" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:transport-defect">15</a> gives $$\Phi(T)\Phi(T')=P L(T)L(T')=P L(TT')=\Phi(TT').$$ The lift preserves adjoints, so $\Phi$ is a $*$-homomorphism on $\mathbb C[B]$. The universal property of the maximal norm extends it to a $*$-homomorphism $A_B\to A_Y$.

<a id="eq:split-approximants"></a>
Let $J:H_Y\to H_B$ be the covering isometry defining $f_A$; it sends the summand at $y\in F_u$ isometrically into the summand at $u$, with orthogonal ranges for distinct $y$ in the same fiber. For $T\in\mathbb C[B]$, continuity gives $$\label{eq:split-approximants}
 f_A\Phi(T)=\lim_{n\to\infty} J P_nL(T)J^*
 \qquad\text{in }A_B.$$ All operators in this limit have propagation at most $\mathop{\mathrm{prop}}(T)$ on $B$, because $P_n$ preserves the fibers. In concrete operator norm they converge to $$JVV^*L(T)J^*=JVT V^*J^*.$$ The isometry $JV:H_B\to H_B$ lies over the identity of $B$, so $JVT V^*J^*$ belongs to $\mathbb C[B]$ and has the same propagation bound. The difference from each approximant in <a href="#eq:split-approximants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:split-approximants">[eq:split-approximants]</a> has this fixed propagation bound as well. Lemma <a href="#ana:fixed-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:fixed-propagation">9</a> promotes the concrete convergence to maximal-norm convergence. Hence <a id="eq:split-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:split-identity}
 f_A\Phi(T)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(JV)(T).$$ By density this identity holds on $A_B$. Finally, Proposition <a href="#ana:functoriality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:functoriality">12</a> says that a covering isometry over the identity induces the identity on $K$-theory. Applying it to $JV$ proves $(f_A\Phi)_*=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:sliding"></a>
### Moving the analytic class to the two ends

The two endpoint maps $i_-f,i_+f:Y\to X$ are connected componentwise by the height slices of $X_j$. The distance between the endpoints grows with $j$, so a single bounded-distance homotopy does not compare their Roe-algebra maps. Instead, we place a copy of an operator at every height and compare this stack with its shift by one height. The common interior copies will cancel on $K$-theory.

<a id="stmt-ana-endpoints"></a>
<div id="ana:endpoints" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16**. *The endpoint maps induce the same homomorphism $$(i_{+,A}f_A)_*=(i_{-,A}f_A)_*:
 K_1(A_Y)\longrightarrow K_1(A_X).$$*

</div>

<a id="eq:successive-slices"></a>
For $-3j\le t\le3j$, let $$s_{j,t}:Y_j\longrightarrow X_j,
 \qquad (u,q)\longmapsto (u,\overline q,t),$$ where $\overline q$ is reduction from $Q_{2j}$ to $Q_{k_j(t)}$. The map is $1$-Lipschitz: it carries both Cayley edges and horizontal matchings to edges or to vertices. Consecutive slices satisfy $$\label{eq:successive-slices}
 d_X(s_{j,t}(y),s_{j,t+1}(y))=1.$$ Choose exact covering isometries $$D_{j,t}:H_{Y_j}\longrightarrow H_{X_j}$$ over these vertex maps. They exist because every vertex summand is a copy of the infinite-dimensional Hilbert space $H_0$. At the endpoints choose them to be the restrictions of the covering isometries defining $i_{-,A}f_A$ and $i_{+,A}f_A$, respectively. For fixed $j$, the ranges of the $D_{j,t}$ for distinct $t$ are orthogonal, since they lie at distinct heights.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Stacks modulo the compact operators

<a id="eq:stack-zero"></a>
<a id="eq:stack-one"></a>
Let $$q:A_X\longrightarrow A_X/\mathcal K_X$$ be the quotient map. If $T\in\mathbb C[Y]$, write $T_j=\chi_{Y_j}T\chi_{Y_j}$. The difference $T-\bigoplus_jT_j$ has finite vertex support: at a fixed propagation bound there are only finitely many cross-component entries. In particular it is compact. Define $$\begin{aligned}
 S_0(T)&=\bigoplus_j\sum_{t=-3j}^{3j-1}
                D_{j,t}T_jD_{j,t}^*,\label{eq:stack-zero}\\
 S_1(T)&=\bigoplus_j\sum_{t=-3j}^{3j-1}
                D_{j,t+1}T_jD_{j,t+1}^*.
                \label{eq:stack-one}
\end{aligned}$$ The number of copies is unbounded, but their ranges are orthogonal. Thus $\|S_a(T)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}$, for $a=0,1$. If $\mathop{\mathrm{prop}}(T)\le R$, the $1$-Lipschitz property gives $\mathop{\mathrm{prop}}(S_a(T))\le R$. Every vertex entry is compact: it is a finite sum of isometric images of compact entries of $T_j$, since each $Y_j$ is finite. Hence $S_a(T)\in\mathbb C[X]$. No bound on the ranks of these compact entries is required in the algebra used here.

<a id="eq:compression-defect"></a>
The maps $S_a$ need not be multiplicative before taking the quotient. For $T,T'\in\mathbb C[Y]$, however, $$\label{eq:compression-defect}
 (TT')_j-T_jT'_j
   =\chi_{Y_j}T(1-\chi_{Y_j})T'\chi_{Y_j}$$ is zero except for finitely many $j$. Each nonzero term is compact on $H_{Y_j}$, since it is a matrix of compact operators over a finite vertex set. Copying it into the finitely many heights of the corresponding $X_j$ remains compact. Orthogonality of the height ranges and <a href="#eq:compression-defect" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compression-defect">[eq:compression-defect]</a> therefore show that $$\overline S_a=qS_a:\mathbb C[Y]\longrightarrow A_X/\mathcal K_X$$ is a $*$-homomorphism. By maximal universality, it extends to a $*$-homomorphism with domain $A_Y$.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### A continuous rotation between the stacks

We next prove that $\overline S_0$ and $\overline S_1$ induce the same map on $K$-theory. The rotation takes place in a doubled target module; this ensures that the ranges stay orthogonal at every intermediate angle.

For $0\le\lambda\le\pi/2$, define $$W_{\lambda,j,t}=
 \begin{pmatrix}
  \cos\lambda\,D_{j,t}\\
  \sin\lambda\,D_{j,t+1}
 \end{pmatrix}
 :H_{Y_j}\longrightarrow H_{X_j}\oplus H_{X_j},
 \qquad -3j\le t<3j.$$ Each $W_{\lambda,j,t}$ is an isometry. For distinct pairs $(j,t)$, their ranges are orthogonal: they lie in different components or, within one component, at different heights in each target copy. In the first copy the heights are $t$, and in the second they are $t+1$. Thus they assemble to an isometry $$W_\lambda:\bigoplus_{j}\bigoplus_{t=-3j}^{3j-1}H_{Y_j}
          \longrightarrow H_X\oplus H_X.$$ On $\mathbb C[Y]$ define <a id="eq:stack-rotation"></a>$$\label{eq:stack-rotation}
 F_\lambda(T)=W_\lambda
       \left(\bigoplus_j\bigoplus_{t=-3j}^{3j-1}T_j\right)
       W_\lambda^*.$$ Its diagonal matrix blocks are scalar multiples of the stacks in <a href="#eq:stack-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stack-zero">[eq:stack-zero]</a>–<a href="#eq:stack-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stack-one">[eq:stack-one]</a>. The off-diagonal blocks are sums of $D_{j,t}T_jD_{j,t+1}^*$ and their counterparts. If $\mathop{\mathrm{prop}}(T)\le R$, then <a href="#eq:successive-slices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:successive-slices">[eq:successive-slices]</a> gives propagation at most $R+1$ for these blocks. Their vertex entries are again finite sums of compact operators. We conclude that $$F_\lambda(T)\in M_2(\mathbb C[X]),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{prop}}(F_\lambda(T))\le R+1.$$ The same calculation as <a href="#eq:compression-defect" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compression-defect">[eq:compression-defect]</a>, using $W_\lambda^*W_\lambda=1$, shows that the images of $F_\lambda$ in $M_2(A_X/\mathcal K_X)$ are $*$-homomorphisms on $\mathbb C[Y]$. Each extends to $A_Y$ by maximal universality.

<a id="eq:rotation-continuity"></a>
For completeness, continuity is uniform in the number of height slices. The orthogonality just established gives $$\|W_\lambda-W_\mu\|
  =\bigl((\cos\lambda-\cos\mu)^2
            +(\sin\lambda-\sin\mu)^2\bigr)^{1/2}
  \le |\lambda-\mu|.$$ Consequently, in concrete operator norm, $$\label{eq:rotation-continuity}
 \|F_\lambda(T)-F_\mu(T)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
       \le2\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,|\lambda-\mu|.$$ The difference has propagation at most $R+1$, independently of the angles. Applying Lemma <a href="#ana:fixed-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:fixed-propagation">9</a> to its four matrix blocks promotes <a href="#eq:rotation-continuity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-continuity">[eq:rotation-continuity]</a> to an estimate $$\|F_\lambda(T)-F_\mu(T)\|_{M_2(A_X)}
       \le C_R\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,|\lambda-\mu|$$ for a constant independent of $j,\lambda,\mu$. This proves point-norm continuity on the dense algebra $\mathbb C[Y]$. The extended homomorphisms are contractive for the maximal norm, so density proves point-norm continuity on all of $A_Y$.

<a id="eq:equal-stacks"></a>
At $\lambda=0$ the homomorphism is $\overline S_0$ in the first matrix corner, and at $\lambda=\pi/2$ it is $\overline S_1$ in the second. The corner identifications on $K$-theory agree, so $$\label{eq:equal-stacks}
 (\overline S_0)_*=(\overline S_1)_*.$$

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Canceling the interior copies

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-endpoints"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-endpoints</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#ana:endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:endpoints">16</a>.* Define the interior stack by $$I(T)=\bigoplus_j\sum_{t=-3j+1}^{3j-1}D_{j,t}T_jD_{j,t}^*.$$ Its image $\overline I=qI$ is a $*$-homomorphism on $A_Y$ by the same compact-defect argument. Let $$E_-=i_{-,A}f_A,\qquad E_+=i_{+,A}f_A.$$ The endpoints in the two stacks agree modulo $\mathcal K_X$ with $E_-$ and $E_+$, respectively. Indeed, our endpoint covering isometries agree with those defining $E_\pm$, and discarding the cross-component part of $T$ changes its image by a compact operator. Hence $$\overline S_0=\overline I+qE_-,\qquad
 \overline S_1=\overline I+qE_+.$$ In each sum, the two homomorphisms have orthogonal ranges: one is supported on the strictly interior heights and the other on an endpoint.

Orthogonal sums induce sums on $K_1$. Explicitly, if $\varphi$ and $\psi$ are orthogonal homomorphisms and $u$ is a matrix unitary with $u-1$ in the domain algebra, then $$1+(\varphi+\psi)(u-1)
   =\bigl(1+\varphi(u-1)\bigr)
      \bigl(1+\psi(u-1)\bigr),$$ and multiplication of unitaries gives addition of their $K_1$-classes. Using this additivity in <a href="#eq:equal-stacks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:equal-stacks">[eq:equal-stacks]</a> and canceling $\overline I_*$ gives $$q_*(E_-)_*=q_*(E_+)_*.$$ By Lemma <a href="#ana:compacts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:compacts">10</a>, $\mathcal K_X$ is the algebra of compact operators on $H_X$, so $K_1(\mathcal K_X)=0$. The six-term exact sequence for $$0\longrightarrow\mathcal K_X\longrightarrow A_X
       \xrightarrow{q}A_X/\mathcal K_X\longrightarrow0$$ therefore makes $q_*:K_1(A_X)\to K_1(A_X/\mathcal K_X)$ injective. It follows that $(E_-)_*=(E_+)_*$, as asserted. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-ana-endpoints-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-ana-endpoints</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The graphs $X_j$ constructed above are finite and connected with a uniform degree bound, and their coarse disjoint union $X$ is uniformly discrete and has bounded geometry. Proposition <a href="#top:infinite-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="top:infinite-order">8</a> gives an infinite-order class $$\alpha=(i_+)_*\beta-(i_-)_*\beta\in\mathop{\mathrm{KX}}_1(X).$$ By Proposition <a href="#ana:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:transfer">14</a>, choose $b\in K_1(A_Y)$ satisfying $$(f_A)_*(b)=\mu_B^{\max}(\beta).$$ Naturality of the maximal coarse index (Proposition <a href="#ana:naturality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:naturality">13</a>) and Proposition <a href="#ana:endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ana:endpoints">16</a> now give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mu_X^{\max}(\alpha)
  &=(i_{+,A})_*\mu_B^{\max}(\beta)
       -(i_{-,A})_*\mu_B^{\max}(\beta)\\
  &=(i_{+,A}f_A)_*(b)-(i_{-,A}f_A)_*(b)=0.
\end{aligned}$$ An infinite-order element remains nonzero after tensoring with $\mathbb Q$, so $\alpha\otimes1$ is a nonzero element of the kernel of the rationalized maximal coarse assembly map. ◻

</details>

```json
  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The congruence level as a function of height in $X_{j}$. Every slice lies over the same discrete torus $B_{j}$. Outside the central collar, each vertical step changes the quotient level by one. The drawing records the level profile, not a geometric embedding of the graph or its fiber cardinalities.",
      "figure_id": "fig:profile",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:profile",
          "exact_quote": "The congruence level as a function of height in $X_j$.\nEvery slice lies over the same discrete torus $B_j$. Outside the central\ncollar, each vertical step changes the quotient level by one. The drawing\nrecords the level profile, not a geome",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.45cm,y=1.05cm,>=Stealth,font=\\small]\n \\fill[blue!7] (-1,-0.08) rectangle (1,2.27);\n \\draw[->,gray] (-3.45,0)--(3.6,0) node[right] {$t$};\n \\draw[very thick,blue!65!black] (-3,0)--(-1,2)--(1,2)--(3,0);\n \\draw[dashed,gray] (-1,0)--(-1,2);\n \\draw[dashed,gray] (1,0)--(1,2);\n \\draw[dashed,gray] (0,0)--(0,2);\n \\foreach \\x/\\lab in {-3/{-3j},-1/{-j},0/{0},1/{j},3/{3j}}{\n  \\draw (\\x,0.05)--(\\x,-0.05);\n  \\node[below] at (\\x,-0.06) {$\\lab$};\n }\n \\fill (-3,0) circle (1.7pt);\n \\fill (3,0) circle (1.7pt);\n \\fill (0,2) circle (1.7pt);\n \\node[above left] at (-3,0.08) {$B_j$};\n \\node[above right] at (3,0.08) {$B_j$};\n \\node[above] at (0,2.07) {$Y_j$ (fiber $Q_{2j}$)};\n \\node at (0,1.08) {central collar};\n \\node[rotate=45,above] at (-2,1) {$Q_{k_j(t)}$};\n \\node[rotate=-45,above] at (2,1) {$Q_{k_j(t)}$};\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-3.35,2) {$k_j(t)=2j$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A 2D plot showing the congruence level profile against height $t$. A horizontal axis labeled $t$ with an arrow to the right contains marked tick values $-3j$, $-j$, $0$, $j$, and $3j$. Labels $B_j$ appear above the axis at $-3j$ and $3j$. The curve forms a symmetric trapezoid: a straight line segment rises from $(-3j, 0)$ to $(-j, 2j)$ labeled $Q_{k_j(t)}$ along the incline; a horizontal segment runs at height $2j$ between $t = -j$ and $t = j$ labeled above by $Y_j \\text{ (fiber } Q_{2j}\\text{)}$; and a straight line segment descends from $(j, 2j)$ to $(3j, 0)$ labeled $Q_{k_j(t)}$ along the decline. On the left side, the height of the flat top is labeled $k_j(t) = 2j$. Two vertical dashed lines connect $t = -j$ to $(-j, 2j)$ and $t = j$ to $(j, 2j)$, with the enclosed region labeled \"central collar\"."
    }
  ],
```

```json
{
  "edges": [
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The maximal-target kernel result strengthens the companion reduced-target kernel result, since the maximal-to-reduced quotient carries maximal indices to reduced indices.",
      "relation": "generalises",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-307.1",
      "source_quote": "There exist finite connected graphs $(X_j)_{j\\geq1}$ with a common finite bound on vertex degree, a coarse disjoint union $X=\\bigsqcup_{j\\geq1}X_j$, and an infinite-order class $\\alpha\\in\\mathop{\\mathrm{KX}}_1(X)$ such that $$\\mu_X^{\\max}(\\alpha)=0.$$ Consequently, $\\mu_X^{\\max}\\otimes\\mathbb Q$ is not injective.",
      "target_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-307.2",
      "target_quote": "There is a uniformly discrete bounded-geometry metric space $X$, which is a coarse disjoint union of finite connected graphs of uniformly bounded degree, and an infinite-order element $\\alpha\\in KX_1(X)$ such that $$\\mu_X(\\alpha)=0\\quad\\text{in }K_1(C^*(X)).$$ Consequently, ordinary coarse assembly for $X$ fails to be injective both integrally and after tensoring its domain and target with $\\mathbb Q$.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both works construct bounded geometry spaces as disjoint unions of finite connected graphs to support infinite order classes whose assembly index vanishes.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-geom-quotients",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-307.1",
      "source_quote": "The graphs $B_j,Y_j,X_j$ are finite and connected, with vertex degrees bounded independently of $j$.",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-307.2",
      "target_quote": "coarse disjoint union of finite connected graphs of uniformly bounded degree",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    }
  ],
```
